```text
24	
25	RSFT CED achieves a three-seed mean of 44.82 Ign-F1 (+1.24 over SFT mean) while compressing variance to 0.51pp, a 7.5$\times$ reduction. This stabilization holds across all three Qwen3 scales (Section~\ref{sec:scaling}). Even the weakest RSFT seed (44.23) exceeds the SFT mean (43.58), and every RSFT seed outperforms two of three SFT seeds. Evidence quality improves (Evi-F1 80.96 vs.\ 79.61) and coupling tightens (EDCR 0.6487 vs.\ 0.6797). CED-based selection thus provides two complementary benefits: it raises mean extraction performance and stabilizes it across training runs. Per-seed results appear in Appendix~\ref{sec:ablation_full}.
26
```

```text
95	A key finding from multi-seed evaluation is that the apparent magnitude of the evidence tax is sensitive to SFT seed variance. At 4B, the three-seed tax is $-3.63$ points, but individual seeds range from $-0$ (s44) to $-7.47$ (s42). The SFT standard deviation of 3.80pp is comparable to the tax itself, meaning single-seed evaluation can substantially overestimate or underestimate the true cost. At 8B, the two-seed mean tax is $-2.13$ (Qwen3-8B) and $-0.68$ for LLaMA-3.1-8B. The most surprising result appears at 1.7B: instead of paying a larger tax, the smallest model \emph{benefits} from evidence generation (+4.82 points, three-seed mean). Individual seeds vary substantially (s42: +8.04, s43: +3.04, s44: +3.39), but all three consistently benefit. The no-evidence model suffers a higher truncation rate, partially inflating the gap; a controlled evaluation on the canonical seed (Appendix~\ref{sec:fair_subset}) confirms the benefit is genuine. We hypothesize that evidence generation provides implicit regularization for small models, forcing attention to supporting context rather than surface-level memorization.
96	
97	RSFT improves all scales while compressing variance. Mean gains are +6.52 for 1.7B, +1.24 for 4B, and +5.48 for 8B. The variance reduction is equally important: at 1.7B, SFT standard deviation is 2.79pp while RSFT achieves 0.09pp (two-seed); at 4B, 3.80pp compresses to 0.51pp (7.5$\times$); at 8B, 2.62pp compresses to 0.53pp (5.0$\times$). RSFT lifts the weakest SFT seed most: the 4B s42 SFT (39.74 Ign-F1) produces RSFT seeds ranging 44.16--45.12, recovering over 4pp that SFT variability lost. At 8B, the RSFT gain is +5.48 rather than the +3.73 observed in single-seed comparison, because multi-seed SFT averaging exposes higher baseline instability. A striking consequence is that 1.7B RSFT achieves 51.16 Ign-F1, surpassing the 8B SFT mean of 48.66 (+2.50pp), enabling a 4.7$\times$ smaller model to outperform a larger one on evidence-grounded extraction.
```

```json
    "newString": "A striking consequence is that 1.7B RSFT (51.16 Ign-F1) is comparable to or exceeds the 8B SFT mean (48.66, +2.50pp difference), suggesting that a 4.7$\\times$ smaller model can reach 8B-level performance when RSFT is applied to evidence-grounded extraction.",

        "lines": [
          " ",
          " A key finding from multi-seed evaluation is that the apparent magnitude of the evidence tax is sensitive to SFT seed variance. At 4B, the three-seed tax is $-3.63$ points, but individual seeds range from $-0$ (s44) to $-7.47$ (s42). The SFT standard deviation of 3.80pp is comparable to the tax itself, meaning single-seed evaluation can substantially overestimate or underestimate the true cost. At 8B, the two-seed mean tax is $-2.13$ (Qwen3-8B) and $-0.68$ for LLaMA-3.1-8B. The most surprising result appears at 1.7B: instead of paying a larger tax, the smallest model \\emph{benefits} from evidence generation (+4.82 points, three-seed mean). Individual seeds vary substantially (s42: +8.04, s43: +3.04, s44: +3.39), but all three consistently benefit. The no-evidence model suffers a higher truncation rate, partially inflating the gap; a controlled evaluation on the canonical seed (Appendix~\\ref{sec:fair_subset}) confirms the benefit is genuine. We hypothesize that evidence generation provides implicit regularization for small models, forcing attention to supporting context rather than surface-level memorization.",
          " ",
          "-RSFT improves all scales while compressing variance. Mean gains are +6.52 for 1.7B, +1.24 for 4B, and +5.48 for 8B. At 1.7B, the two RSFT seeds differ by only 0.13pp against an SFT std of 2.79pp ($n{=}3$); additional seeds are needed to quantify the compression ratio reliably. RSFT lifts the weakest SFT seed most: the 4B s42 SFT (39.74 Ign-F1) produces RSFT seeds ranging 44.16--45.12, recovering over 4pp that SFT variability lost. At 8B, the RSFT gain is +5.48 rather than the +3.73 observed in single-seed comparison, because multi-seed SFT averaging exposes higher baseline instability. A striking consequence is that 1.7B RSFT achieves 51.16 Ign-F1, surpassing the 8B SFT mean of 48.66 (+2.50pp), enabling a 4.7$\\times$ smaller model to outperform a larger one on evidence-grounded extraction.",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{EDCR as a diagnostic.}\nEDCR (\\S\\ref{sec:edcr}) operates at two complementary levels. Cross-method, Spearman $\\rho = -0.89$ ($n = 7$, $p = 0.007$) across seven independent method configurations (Table~\\ref{tab:spectrum}). Removing the extreme GRPO QLoRA outlier yields $\\rho = -0.83$ ($n = 6$, $p = 0.042$), indicating that GRPO QLoRA is a high-leverage point. Per-relation, $\\rho = -0.79$ ($n = 76$, $p < 0.0001$) across relation types provides robust validation independent of method-level sample size. EDCR shows no correlation with Evi-F1 ($\\rho = -0.06$, $p = 0.58$), confirming it captures information orthogonal to evidence quality (Appendix~\\ref{sec:edcr_per_relation}). RSFT avoids decoupling because discrete output selection prevents trading extraction for evidence quality (Appendix~\\ref{sec:rsft_theory}). We provide qualitative examples of these phenomena in Appendix~\\ref{sec:qualitative}.\n\n\\paragraph{CED granularity.}\nCED operates effectively at the response level for both RSFT selection and inference-time reranking, but fails at sentence-level granularity: filtering individual evidence sentences by NLI score reduces Evi-F1 by 7.7pp (Recall $-$20pp), as it disrupts co-reference chains across evidence sentences. This suggests that evidence quality is a holistic response property rather than a per-sentence attribute.\n\n\\paragraph{Ablation.}\nCED and flat-NLI produce comparable performance (Rel-F1 45.63 vs.\\ 45.61); stricter selection ($k{=}1$) outperforms $k{=}3$ by 1.02 Ign-F1 despite 2.96$\\times$ fewer examples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:ablation_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Error pattern shifts.}\nRSFT reduces truncation FN ($-16$ to $-22$\\%), hallucinated FP ($-6$ to $-12$\\%), and duplicates ($-11$ to $-20$\\%) consistently across scales (Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_analysis}). However, evidence quality is unchanged ($\\Delta < 0.4$pp), indicating that RSFT improves \\emph{what} the model extracts but not \\emph{how well} it grounds extractions.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical implications.}\nOur findings suggest three guidelines for practitioners seeking evidence-grounded IE: (1) avoid online RL for multi-objective generation tasks where capability coupling matters; (2) prefer RSFT with strict selection ($k{=}1$) over training data quantity, as it both improves mean performance and compresses seed variance by 5$\\times$ or more; (3) consider CED reranking at inference for deployment scenarios requiring both high extraction accuracy and evidence quality. The variance compression is particularly relevant for production deployment: SFT training seeds can shift Ign-F1 by $\\pm$3.80pp at 4B, while RSFT limits this to $\\pm$0.51pp, reducing the risk that an unlucky seed produces a substantially weaker model.\n",
```

_source line 4847 · 2026-05-19T11:12:35.295Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch] 1.7B 3-seed 最终数据 + Reviewer 概念性修正（重要）

## 最终 1.7B 数据
- s42=0.5122, s43=0.5109, s44=0.5125
- Mean=0.5119, Std=0.085pp, Range=0.0016
- evi_f1=0.8267 (s44), EDCR=0.6181 (s44)

## Reviewer 概念性修正（必须执行）

### 1. Variance compression 表述区分（critical）
当前 "variance compression" 叙事有概念问题：Method A 的 RSFT variance 反映的是 **RSFT 训练随机性**（固定 SFT base，不同 RSFT seed），而 SFT variance 反映的是 **SFT 训练随机性**（不同 SFT seed）。这两种 variance 来源不同，直接比较 "7.5x compression" 存在概念偷换。

**修正方向**：
- 可以 claim："RSFT training is highly reproducible regardless of random seed"（RSFT 训练稳定性）
- 不可以 claim："RSFT produces more robust final models than SFT end-to-end"（端到端稳定性）——后者需要 Method B 数据（D112 bf16 重跑中）
- 论文中所有 "variance compression Nx" 处，明确说明：这比较的是 "cross-SFT-seed variance" vs "cross-RSFT-seed variance (from shared SFT base)"，读者需知晓两者来源不同
- 建议措辞示例："While SFT models show substantial variation across training seeds (std=2.79pp at 1.7B), RSFT training from the same SFT checkpoint produces remarkably consistent results (std=0.085pp), though we note these two sources of variance are not directly comparable."

### 2. "surpasses" → "outperforms in all observed comparisons"
1.7B RSFT 最低值 (s43=0.5109) > 8B SFT 最高值 (s42=0.5051)，差 +0.58pp。Welch's t-test p=0.20（不显著）。

改为："every 1.7B RSFT seed outperforms every observed 8B SFT seed (+0.58pp minimum gap), suggesting a 4.7× smaller model can match or exceed 8B-level performance with RSFT"

### 3. Table 更新
- 1.7B RSFT 行：51.16±0.09^(2) → 51.19±0.09^(3)
- 确认 4B SFT s42 D076 权威值 = 0.3974（D111 裁决），3-seed mean = 0.4358

这次不用验证引用。

```text
1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\paragraph{EDCR as a diagnostic.}
5	EDCR (\S\ref{sec:edcr}) operates at two complementary levels. Cross-method, Spearman $\rho = -0.89$ ($n = 7$, $p = 0.007$) across seven independent method configurations (Table~\ref{tab:spectrum}). Removing the extreme GRPO QLoRA outlier yields $\rho = -0.83$ ($n = 6$, $p = 0.042$), indicating that GRPO QLoRA is a high-leverage point. Per-relation, $\rho = -0.79$ ($n = 76$, $p < 0.0001$) across relation types provides robust validation independent of method-level sample size. EDCR shows no correlation with Evi-F1 ($\rho = -0.06$, $p = 0.58$), confirming it captures information orthogonal to evidence quality (Appendix~\ref{sec:edcr_per_relation}). RSFT avoids decoupling because discrete output selection prevents trading extraction for evidence quality (Appendix~\ref{sec:rsft_theory}). We provide qualitative examples of these phenomena in Appendix~\ref{sec:qualitative}.
6	
7	\paragraph{CED granularity.}
8	CED operates effectively at the response level for both RSFT selection and inference-time reranking, but fails at sentence-level granularity: filtering individual evidence sentences by NLI score reduces Evi-F1 by 7.7pp (Recall $-$20pp), as it disrupts co-reference chains across evidence sentences. This suggests that evidence quality is a holistic response property rather than a per-sentence attribute.
9	
10	\paragraph{Ablation.}
11	CED and flat-NLI produce comparable performance (Rel-F1 45.63 vs.\ 45.61); stricter selection ($k{=}1$) outperforms $k{=}3$ by 1.02 Ign-F1 despite 2.96$\times$ fewer examples (Appendix~\ref{sec:ablation_full}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Error pattern shifts.}
14	RSFT reduces truncation FN ($-16$ to $-22$\%), hallucinated FP ($-6$ to $-12$\%), and duplicates ($-11$ to $-20$\%) consistently across scales (Appendix~\ref{sec:error_analysis}). However, evidence quality is unchanged ($\Delta < 0.4$pp), indicating that RSFT improves \emph{what} the model extracts but not \emph{how well} it grounds extractions.
15	
16	\paragraph{Practical implications.}
17	Our findings suggest three guidelines for practitioners seeking evidence-grounded IE: (1) avoid online RL for multi-objective generation tasks where capability coupling matters; (2) prefer RSFT with strict selection ($k{=}1$) over training data quantity, as it both improves mean performance and compresses seed variance (7.5$\times$ at 4B, 5.0$\times$ at 8B); (3) consider CED reranking at inference for deployment scenarios requiring both high extraction accuracy and evidence quality. The variance compression is particularly relevant for production deployment: SFT training seeds can shift Ign-F1 by $\pm$3.80pp at 4B, while RSFT limits this to $\pm$0.51pp, reducing the risk that an unlucky seed produces a substantially weaker model.
18	
```

RSFT lifts the weakest SFT seed most: the 4B s42 SFT (39.74 Ign-F1) produces RSFT seeds ranging 44.16--45.12, recovering over 4pp that SFT variability lost. At 8B, the RSFT gain is +5.48 rather than the +3.73 observed in single-seed comparison, because multi-seed SFT averaging exposes higher baseline instability. A striking consequence is that 1.7B RSFT (51.16 Ign-F1) is comparable to or exceeds the 8B SFT mean (48.66, +2.50pp difference), suggesting that a 4.7$\\times$ smaller model can reach 8B-level performance when RSFT is applied to evidence-grounded extraction.",
```json
  "new_string": "RSFT improves mean performance at all scales: +6.55 for 1.7B, +1.24 for 4B, and +5.48 for 8B. RSFT training from a shared SFT checkpoint is also highly reproducible: three seeds yield std 0.09pp at 1.7B ($n{=}3$), 0.51pp at 4B ($n{=}3$), and 0.53pp at 8B ($n{=}2$). While SFT models show substantially larger cross-seed variation (std 2.79pp at 1.7B, 3.80pp at 4B, 2.62pp at 8B), these two sources of variance are not directly comparable: SFT variance reflects full training runs from different random seeds, whereas RSFT variance reflects only the additional fine-tuning stage from a fixed SFT base. RSFT lifts the weakest SFT seed most: the 4B s42 SFT (39.74 Ign-F1) produces RSFT seeds ranging 44.16--45.12, recovering over 4pp that SFT variability lost. At 8B, the RSFT gain is +5.48 rather than the +3.73 observed in single-seed comparison, because multi-seed SFT averaging exposes higher baseline instability. Every 1.7B RSFT seed outperforms every observed 8B SFT seed (+0.58pp minimum gap), suggesting that a 4.7$\\times$ smaller model can match or exceed 8B-level performance with RSFT applied to evidence-grounded extraction."
}
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{EDCR as a diagnostic.}\nEDCR (\\S\\ref{sec:edcr}) operates at two complementary levels. Cross-method, Spearman $\\rho = -0.89$ ($n = 7$, $p = 0.007$) across seven independent method configurations (Table~\\ref{tab:spectrum}). Removing the extreme GRPO QLoRA outlier yields $\\rho = -0.83$ ($n = 6$, $p = 0.042$), indicating that GRPO QLoRA is a high-leverage point. Per-relation, $\\rho = -0.79$ ($n = 76$, $p < 0.0001$) across relation types provides robust validation independent of method-level sample size. EDCR shows no correlation with Evi-F1 ($\\rho = -0.06$, $p = 0.58$), confirming it captures information orthogonal to evidence quality (Appendix~\\ref{sec:edcr_per_relation}). RSFT avoids decoupling because discrete output selection prevents trading extraction for evidence quality (Appendix~\\ref{sec:rsft_theory}). We provide qualitative examples of these phenomena in Appendix~\\ref{sec:qualitative}.\n\n\\paragraph{CED granularity.}\nCED operates effectively at the response level for both RSFT selection and inference-time reranking, but fails at sentence-level granularity: filtering individual evidence sentences by NLI score reduces Evi-F1 by 7.7pp (Recall $-$20pp), as it disrupts co-reference chains across evidence sentences. This suggests that evidence quality is a holistic response property rather than a per-sentence attribute.\n\n\\paragraph{Ablation.}\nCED and flat-NLI produce comparable performance (Rel-F1 45.63 vs.\\ 45.61); stricter selection ($k{=}1$) outperforms $k{=}3$ by 1.02 Ign-F1 despite 2.96$\\times$ fewer examples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:ablation_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Error pattern shifts.}\nRSFT reduces truncation FN ($-16$ to $-22$\\%), hallucinated FP ($-6$ to $-12$\\%), and duplicates ($-11$ to $-20$\\%) consistently across scales (Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_analysis}). However, evidence quality is unchanged ($\\Delta < 0.4$pp), indicating that RSFT improves \\emph{what} the model extracts but not \\emph{how well} it grounds extractions.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical implications.}\nOur findings suggest three guidelines for practitioners seeking evidence-grounded IE: (1) avoid online RL for multi-objective generation tasks where capability coupling matters; (2) prefer RSFT with strict selection ($k{=}1$) over training data quantity, as it both improves mean performance and compresses seed variance (7.5$\\times$ at 4B, 5.0$\\times$ at 8B); (3) consider CED reranking at inference for deployment scenarios requiring both high extraction accuracy and evidence quality. The variance compression is particularly relevant for production deployment: SFT training seeds can shift Ign-F1 by $\\pm$3.80pp at 4B, while RSFT limits this to $\\pm$0.51pp, reducing the risk that an unlucky seed produces a substantially weaker model.\n",

      {

          " RSFT reduces truncation FN ($-16$ to $-22$\\%), hallucinated FP ($-6$ to $-12$\\%), and duplicates ($-11$ to $-20$\\%) consistently across scales (Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_analysis}). However, evidence quality is unchanged ($\\Delta < 0.4$pp), indicating that RSFT improves \\emph{what} the model extracts but not \\emph{how well} it grounds extractions.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Practical implications.}",
          "-Our findings suggest three guidelines for practitioners seeking evidence-grounded IE: (1) avoid online RL for multi-objective generation tasks where capability coupling matters; (2) prefer RSFT with strict selection ($k{=}1$) over training data quantity, as it both improves mean performance and compresses seed variance (7.5$\\times$ at 4B, 5.0$\\times$ at 8B); (3) consider CED reranking at inference for deployment scenarios requiring both high extraction accuracy and evidence quality. The variance compression is particularly relevant for production deployment: SFT training seeds can shift Ign-F1 by $\\pm$3.80pp at 4B, while RSFT limits this to $\\pm$0.51pp, reducing the risk that an unlucky seed produces a substantially weaker model.",
          "+Our findings suggest three guidelines for practitioners seeking evidence-grounded IE: (1) avoid online RL for multi-objective generation tasks where capability coupling matters; (2) prefer RSFT with strict selection ($k{=}1$) over training data quantity, as it both improves mean performance and yields highly reproducible training (std $\\leq$0.53pp at 4B and 8B from a fixed SFT base); (3) consider CED reranking at inference for deployment scenarios requiring both high extraction accuracy and evidence quality. For production deployment, SFT training seeds can shift Ign-F1 by $\\pm$3.80pp at 4B, whereas RSFT training from a given checkpoint varies by only $\\pm$0.51pp, though these measure different sources of variance (cross-SFT-seed vs.\\ cross-RSFT-seed from a shared base)."
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01He7dgMtUzzPWf3XRA2kMhh`</summary>

```text
95	A key finding from multi-seed evaluation is that the apparent magnitude of the evidence tax is sensitive to SFT seed variance. At 4B, the three-seed tax is $-3.63$ points, but individual seeds range from $-0$ (s44) to $-7.47$ (s42). The SFT standard deviation of 3.80pp is comparable to the tax itself, meaning single-seed evaluation can substantially overestimate or underestimate the true cost. At 8B, the two-seed mean tax is $-2.13$ (Qwen3-8B) and $-0.68$ for LLaMA-3.1-8B. The most surprising result appears at 1.7B: instead of paying a larger tax, the smallest model \emph{benefits} from evidence generation (+4.82 points, three-seed mean). Individual seeds vary substantially (s42: +8.04, s43: +3.04, s44: +3.39), but all three consistently benefit. The no-evidence model suffers a higher truncation rate, partially inflating the gap; a controlled evaluation on the canonical seed (Appendix~\ref{sec:fair_subset}) confirms the benefit is genuine. We hypothesize that evidence generation provides implicit regularization for small models, forcing attention to supporting context rather than surface-level memorization.
96	
97	RSFT improves mean performance at all scales: +6.55 for 1.7B, +1.24 for 4B, and +5.48 for 8B. RSFT training from a shared SFT checkpoint is also highly reproducible: three seeds yield std 0.09pp at 1.7B ($n{=}3$), 0.51pp at 4B ($n{=}3$), and 0.53pp at 8B ($n{=}2$). While SFT models show substantially larger cross-seed variation (std 2.79pp at 1.7B, 3.80pp at 4B, 2.62pp at 8B), these two sources of variance are not directly comparable: SFT variance reflects full training runs from different random seeds, whereas RSFT variance reflects only the additional fine-tuning stage from a fixed SFT base. RSFT lifts the weakest SFT seed most: the 4B s42 SFT (39.74 Ign-F1) produces RSFT seeds ranging 44.16--45.12, recovering over 4pp that SFT variability lost. At 8B, the RSFT gain is +5.48 rather than the +3.73 observed in single-seed comparison, because multi-seed SFT averaging exposes higher baseline instability. Every 1.7B RSFT seed outperforms every observed 8B SFT seed (+0.58pp minimum gap), suggesting that a 4.7$\times$ smaller model can match or exceed 8B-level performance with RSFT applied to evidence-grounded extraction.
98	
99	\begin{table}[t]
```

```text
105	\section{Controlled Evaluation Subset}
106	\label{sec:fair_subset}
107	
108	The 1.7B evidence tax comparison (\S\ref{sec:scaling}) is confounded by differential output quality: the no-evidence model truncates 50.9\% of outputs (501/985 documents) at the \texttt{max\_new\_tokens} limit, versus 31.9\% (314/985) for the evidence model. To control for this, we define a \emph{controlled evaluation subset} that excludes any document where either model produces a truncated or repetitive output. An output is classified as \emph{truncated} if it reaches the generation length limit. An output is classified as \emph{repetitive} if it contains $\geq$3 consecutive identical triples or if the ratio of unique triples to total triples falls below 0.5 (when total $> 5$). The resulting subset contains 442 of 985 development documents (44.9\%). On this subset, the canonical (s42) evidence tax is +6.2pp (vs.\ +8.04pp on the full set for s42; multi-seed mean tax is +4.82pp), indicating that truncation accounts for approximately 23\% of the s42 gap. The positive direction is preserved, confirming that the 1.7B evidence benefit is genuine and not an artifact of output format differences.
109	
110	Table~\ref{tab:mechanistic} provides a mechanistic breakdown across three scales. Output duplication rate (fraction of documents containing $\geq$3 repeated triples or unique/total $< 0.5$) declines monotonically with scale for both training configurations, but the gap between no-evidence and with-evidence models narrows from 27.5pp at 1.7B to 3.7pp at 8B. This pattern supports the implicit regularization hypothesis: evidence generation forces smaller models to attend to supporting context, reducing degenerate repetition. At 4B, duplication is already moderate (31.1\% with evidence), so the regularization benefit diminishes, while the model lacks sufficient capacity to jointly optimize extraction and evidence generation, creating the capacity valley that produces the largest evidence tax ($-3.63$pp, three-seed SFT mean). The truncation asymmetry reverses across scales: at 1.7B the no-evidence model truncates 19pp more often; at 8B the evidence model truncates 9pp more often due to the longer output format.
111	
112	\begin{table}[t]
```

```text
22	
23	Table~\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison on Qwen3-4B. The SFT baseline exhibits high seed sensitivity: three-seed mean Ign-F1 is 43.58 with standard deviation 3.80pp (individual seeds range from 39.74 to 47.34). This variance is comparable to the evidence tax itself ($-3.63$ points), indicating that single-seed evaluation can substantially misestimate the true cost of joint evidence generation. Removing evidence entirely (no-evidence SFT) reaches 47.21 Ign-F1.
24	
25	RSFT CED achieves a three-seed mean of 44.82 Ign-F1 (+1.24 over SFT mean). RSFT training from a fixed SFT checkpoint is highly reproducible (std 0.51pp across three seeds), contrasting with the large cross-seed SFT variance (std 3.80pp), though these reflect different sources of variation (Section~\ref{sec:scaling}). Even the weakest RSFT seed (44.23) exceeds the SFT mean (43.58), and every RSFT seed outperforms two of three SFT seeds. Evidence quality improves (Evi-F1 80.96 vs.\ 79.61) and coupling tightens (EDCR 0.6487 vs.\ 0.6797). CED-based selection thus provides two complementary benefits: it raises mean extraction performance and stabilizes it across training runs. Per-seed results appear in Appendix~\ref{sec:ablation_full}.
26	
27	The remaining gap to the no-evidence upper bound (2.39 points) represents the residual cost of producing structured evidence, a cost that varies with model scale (Section~\ref{sec:scaling}).
28	
29	\begin{table}[t]
```

```text
Generating markdown table... Generating the results table... Looking at the performance metrics across different model sizes, I'm noticing how sensitive the evidence penalty is to random seed variation—at 4B the three-seed average shows a -3.63 point cost, but individual runs swing from essentially zero to -7.47 depending on which seed was used, and that standard deviation of 3.80 points is substantial enough that a single evaluation could give a misleading picture of the true impact.

The pattern shifts dramatically at smaller scales: the 1.7B model actually *improves* when generating evidence, gaining about 4.82 points on average across three seeds, with all individual runs showing consistent gains despite their variation. The no-evidence baseline suffers from higher truncation rates that artificially inflate this advantage, though a controlled comparison on the canonical seed confirms the benefit holds up. I'm thinking this might reflect how evidence generation acts as an implicit regularization mechanism for smaller models, steering them toward grounding their outputs in the provided context rather than relying on surface-level pattern matching. from s42 paired comparisons (the only seed with no-evidence evaluation). The evidence tax shows non-monotonic scaling behavior: it actually helps at 1.7B by 8 points, substantially hurts at 4B by about 7.5 points, and is nearly neutral at 8B. These are single-seed estimates though, and the 4B penalty might be overstated since s42 is the weakest baseline at that scale compared to the other seeds. I need multi-seed no-evidence results to get reliable tax estimates. The 1.7B gain is partially inflated by different truncation rates, but a controlled fair subset evaluation confirms a genuine benefit of around 6.2 points. I'm thinking evidence generation might provide implicit regularization for smaller models, pushing them to focus on supporting context instead of just memorizing surface patterns. I'm going ahead with all the edits now.
```

```json
{
```

```json
  "old_string": "A key finding from multi-seed evaluation is that the apparent magnitude of the evidence tax is sensitive to SFT seed variance. At 4B, the three-seed tax is $-3.63$ points, but individual seeds range from $-0$ (s44) to $-7.47$ (s42). The SFT standard deviation of 3.80pp is comparable to the tax itself, meaning single-seed evaluation can substantially overestimate or underestimate the true cost. At 8B, the two-seed mean tax is $-2.13$ (Qwen3-8B) and $-0.68$ for LLaMA-3.1-8B. The most surprising result appears at 1.7B: instead of paying a larger tax, the smallest model \\emph{benefits} from evidence generation (+4.82 points, three-seed mean). Individual seeds vary substantially (s42: +8.04, s43: +3.04, s44: +3.39), but all three consistently benefit. The no-evidence model suffers a higher truncation rate, partially inflating the gap; a controlled evaluation on the canonical seed (Appendix~\\ref{sec:fair_subset}) confirms the benefit is genuine. We hypothesize that evidence generation provides implicit regularization for small models, forcing attention to supporting context rather than surface-level memorization.",
  "new_string": "Table~\\ref{tab:scaling} reports evidence taxes from s42 paired comparisons (the only seed with no-evidence evaluation). The tax is non-monotonic with scale: evidence generation \\emph{helps} at 1.7B (+8.04pp), substantially hurts at 4B ($-7.47$pp), and is near-neutral at 8B ($-0.28$pp for Qwen3, $-0.68$pp for LLaMA). These are single-seed estimates, and the 4B tax may be inflated because s42 is the weakest SFT seed at that scale (39.74 Ign-F1 vs.\\ 43.65 and 47.34 for s43 and s44); multi-seed no-evidence evaluation is needed for reliable tax estimates. The 1.7B benefit is partially inflated by differential truncation rates; a controlled evaluation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:fair_subset}) confirms a genuine benefit of +6.2pp on a fair subset. We hypothesize that evidence generation provides implicit regularization for small models, forcing attention to supporting context rather than surface-level memorization."
}
```

```json
          " \\section{Controlled Evaluation Subset}",
          " \\label{sec:fair_subset}",
          " ",
          "-The 1.7B evidence tax comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}) is confounded by differential output quality: the no-evidence model truncates 50.9\\% of outputs (501/985 documents) at the \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens} limit, versus 31.9\\% (314/985) for the evidence model. To control for this, we define a \\emph{controlled evaluation subset} that excludes any document where either model produces a truncated or repetitive output. An output is classified as \\emph{truncated} if it reaches the generation length limit. An output is classified as \\emph{repetitive} if it contains $\\geq$3 consecutive identical triples or if the ratio of unique triples to total triples falls below 0.5 (when total $> 5$). The resulting subset contains 442 of 985 development documents (44.9\\%). On this subset, the canonical (s42) evidence tax is +6.2pp (vs.\\ +8.04pp on the full set for s42; multi-seed mean tax is +4.82pp), indicating that truncation accounts for approximately 23\\% of the s42 gap. The positive direction is preserved, confirming that the 1.7B evidence benefit is genuine and not an artifact of output format differences.",
          "+The 1.7B evidence tax comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}) is confounded by differential output quality: the no-evidence model truncates 50.9\\% of outputs (501/985 documents) at the \\texttt{max\\_new\\_tokens} limit, versus 31.9\\% (314/985) for the evidence model. To control for this, we define a \\emph{controlled evaluation subset} that excludes any document where either model produces a truncated or repetitive output. An output is classified as \\emph{truncated} if it reaches the generation length limit. An output is classified as \\emph{repetitive} if it contains $\\geq$3 consecutive identical triples or if the ratio of unique triples to total triples falls below 0.5 (when total $> 5$). The resulting subset contains 442 of 985 development documents (44.9\\%). On this subset, the s42 evidence tax is +6.2pp (vs.\\ +8.04pp on the full set), indicating that truncation accounts for approximately 23\\% of the gap. The positive direction is preserved, confirming that the 1.7B evidence benefit is genuine and not an artifact of output format differences.",
          " ",
          " Table~\\ref{tab:mechanistic} provides a mechanistic breakdown across three scales. Output duplication rate (fraction of documents containing $\\geq$3 repeated triples or unique/total $< 0.5$) declines monotonically with scale for both training configurations, but the gap between no-evidence and with-evidence models narrows from 27.5pp at 1.7B to 3.7pp at 8B. This pattern supports the implicit regularization hypothesis: evidence generation forces smaller models to attend to supporting context, reducing degenerate repetition. At 4B, duplication is already moderate (31.1\\% with evidence), so the regularization benefit diminishes, while the model lacks sufficient capacity to jointly optimize extraction and evidence generation, creating the capacity valley that produces the largest evidence tax ($-3.63$pp, three-seed SFT mean). The truncation asymmetry reverses across scales: at 1.7B the no-evidence model truncates 19pp more often; at 8B the evidence model truncates 9pp more often due to the longer output format.",
          " "
        ]
      }
```

_source line 4937 · 2026-05-19T12:07:41.044Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011QLeQE62PaSsePVZVd7nLX`</summary>

```text
25	RSFT CED achieves a three-seed mean of 44.82 Ign-F1 (+1.24 over SFT mean). RSFT training from a fixed SFT checkpoint is highly reproducible (std 0.51pp across three seeds), contrasting with the large cross-seed SFT variance (std 3.80pp), though these reflect different sources of variation (Section~\ref{sec:scaling}). Even the weakest RSFT seed (44.23) exceeds the SFT mean (43.58), and every RSFT seed outperforms two of three SFT seeds. Evidence quality improves (Evi-F1 80.96 vs.\ 79.61) and coupling tightens (EDCR 0.6487 vs.\ 0.6797). CED-based selection thus provides two complementary benefits: it raises mean extraction performance and stabilizes it across training runs. Per-seed results appear in Appendix~\ref{sec:ablation_full}.
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We formalize evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction as a structured generation task for LLMs and compare three post-training paradigms across 85+ configurations. RL optimization pressure is associated with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic under QLoRA, DPO gradual, RSFT none), and the cost of joint evidence production varies across scales (+6.2pp at 1.7B on a controlled subset, $-$7.5pp at 4B, near zero at 8B). RSFT with NLI-based scoring yields +4.92pp Ign-F1 at 4B (four-seed mean); our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT (50.51) at a fraction of the cost.
5
```

_source line 4967 · 2026-05-19T18:17:14.031Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019L3TdHgyAnHtjt768AErG1`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	
3	Large Language Model (LLM) based Document-level Relation Extraction (DocRE) methods now match or exceed encoder baselines in extraction accuracy, yet they produce bare relation triplets without identifying the sentences that support each prediction.
4	In deployment contexts such as clinical knowledge bases and legal due-diligence systems, users cannot verify predictions without manually locating textual evidence across multi-page documents.
5	Encoder-based evidence methods (SAIS, DREEAM) can produce supporting sentences but trail LLM approaches in accuracy. The community therefore faces a gap between extractive precision and output verifiability.
6	
7	We formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT).
8	Our study spans 85+ experimental configurations across four model scales (1.7B to 8B parameters), two model families, and two datasets.
9	We use the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic tool and NLI-based scoring for sample selection.
10	
11	Three findings emerge.
12	First, RL optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity: GRPO causes catastrophic decoupling, DPO shows gradual degradation, and RSFT preserves stable joint performance.
13	Second, the cost of evidence production varies across model scales: at 1.7B parameters evidence provides a +6.2pp Ign-F1 benefit on a controlled evaluation subset excluding truncated and repetitive outputs, at 4B it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty, and at 8B the cost approaches zero.
14	Third, RSFT with NLI-based scoring is the most effective route: it achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over supervised fine-tuning at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT performance (50.51).
15	
16	\end{abstract}
17	
```

The community therefore faces a gap between extractive precision and output verifiability.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT).\nOur study spans 85+ experimental configurations across four model scales (1.7B to 8B parameters), two model families, and two datasets.\nWe use the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic tool and NLI-based scoring for sample selection.\n\nThree findings emerge.\nFirst, RL optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity: GRPO causes catastrophic decoupling, DPO shows gradual degradation, and RSFT preserves stable joint performance.\nSecond, the cost of evidence production varies across model scales: at 1.7B parameters evidence provides a +6.2pp Ign-F1 benefit on a controlled evaluation subset excluding truncated and repetitive outputs, at 4B it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty, and at 8B the cost approaches zero.\nThird, RSFT with NLI-based scoring is the most effective route: it achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over supervised fine-tuning at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT performance (50.51).\n\n\\end{abstract}\n",

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Document-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.
4	Recent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition for end-to-end extraction, \citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.
5	These methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy.
6	Yet every one of them outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without any indication of \emph{which sentences} in the document support each prediction.
7	In deployment contexts where predictions feed into clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, or financial compliance systems, end users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.
8	
9	The absence of verifiable evidence creates a practical bottleneck.
10	Relation extraction outputs are consumed downstream by knowledge graph construction and human decision workflows.
11	When a system predicts that a drug interacts with a gene, or that a contract clause implies a liability, practitioners must trace each prediction back to source text before acting on it.
12	Without machine-generated evidence, this verification requires exhaustive manual review of the input document for every predicted relation.
13	Encoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.
14	Both improve extraction quality \emph{and} produce evidence annotations.
15	However, encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in extraction accuracy, and their evidence mechanisms (binary sentence classification) do not transfer to generative architectures.
16	The community therefore faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while the evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.
17	
18	This gap reflects a deeper structural difference between evidence in encoder and generative frameworks.
19	In SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is a binary classification task (does sentence $s_i$ support relation $r$?) that serves as auxiliary supervision.
20	The evidence signal \emph{regularizes} the encoder and improves extraction.
21	In LLM-based DocRE, evidence-grounded extraction requires the model to \emph{generate} complete evidence sentences as part of its output sequence, substantially increasing output length and decoding complexity.
22	Evidence production in this setting is not an auxiliary signal but an additional generation burden that competes with the extraction objective for model capacity.
23	Whether this generation cost necessarily harms extraction, or whether the interaction is more nuanced, remains an open question: the answer may depend on model scale and optimization strategy.
24	This uncertainty motivates a systematic investigation of how evidence production interacts with extraction quality across model scales and optimization paradigms.
25	
26	We formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task for LLMs and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: online reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}), offline preference optimization via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO; \citealt{rafailov2023direct}), and offline reinforcement learning via Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT, building on STaR; \citealt{zelikman2022star}).
27	Our experiments across 85+ configurations, spanning four model scales (1.7B to 8B), two model families, and two datasets, reveal three principal findings.
28	\textbf{First}, we identify a \emph{decoupling spectrum}: the strength of RL optimization pressure correlates with the severity of evidence-extraction decoupling.
29	GRPO with Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA; \citealt{hu2022lora}) produces catastrophic decoupling where extraction accuracy collapses to near zero; GRPO with full-precision training shows mild decoupling; DPO exhibits gradual degradation; and RSFT maintains stable joint performance.
30	Catastrophic extraction collapse co-occurs with QLoRA-based GRPO training, though we note that GRPO with full-precision training (bf16, $G{=}8$) achieves EDCR below the SFT baseline, indicating that the decoupling is not inherent to online RL when sufficient precision is available.
31	\textbf{Second}, we discover a \emph{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of jointly producing evidence and relations varies across model scales.
32	At 1.7B parameters, evidence generation provides an implicit regularization benefit of +6.2pp Ign-F1 on a controlled evaluation subset; at 4B, it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty; at 8B, the tax approaches zero.
33	This pattern challenges the assumption that evidence generation uniformly harms extraction.
34	\textbf{Third}, RSFT with NLI-based scoring for training data selection emerges as the most effective optimization route.
35	By selecting high-quality training samples through output filtering rather than applying gradient-based optimization pressure, RSFT sidesteps the multi-objective competition that destabilizes RL and preference methods.
36	
37	Our contributions are as follows:
38	\begin{enumerate}
39	    \item We formalize \textbf{evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction} as a generation task for LLMs and conduct the first \textbf{comprehensive empirical study} spanning 85+ experiments across four model scales, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms.
40	
41	    \item We characterize a \textbf{decoupling spectrum} across optimization paradigms: RL optimization pressure is associated with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We use the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR), a diagnostic that tracks misalignment between extraction and evidence capabilities, as an analytical tool throughout our study. EDCR shares Rel-Recall with Rel-F1 by construction; we interpret the observed cross-method correlation ($\rho = -0.89$, $n = 7$) as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent finding, and validate through per-relation analysis ($\rho = -0.79$, $n = 76$) that avoids the shared-component concern.
42	
43	    \item We identify a \textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of joint evidence production varies across 1.7B, 4B, and 8B parameters, challenging the assumption that evidence generation uniformly degrades extraction quality.
44	
45	    \item We establish \textbf{RSFT with NLI-based scoring} as an effective solution, using CED or flat-NLI to select high-quality training data. RSFT achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over SFT at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT performance (50.51), enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction at reduced computational cost.
46	\end{enumerate}
47
```

```text
90	\subsection{Scale-Dependent Evidence Tax}
91	\label{sec:scaling}
92	
93	The evidence tax, defined as the Ign-F1 difference between no-evidence and joint-evidence SFT, varies across model scales and training seeds. Table~\ref{tab:scaling} presents multi-seed results across four models (Figure~\ref{fig:scaling} in Appendix).
94	
95	Table~\ref{tab:scaling} reports evidence taxes from s42 paired comparisons (the only seed with no-evidence evaluation). The tax is non-monotonic with scale: evidence generation \emph{helps} at 1.7B (+8.04pp), substantially hurts at 4B ($-7.47$pp), and is near-neutral at 8B ($-0.28$pp for Qwen3, $-0.68$pp for LLaMA). These are single-seed estimates, and the 4B tax may be inflated because s42 is the weakest SFT seed at that scale (39.74 Ign-F1 vs.\ 43.65 and 47.34 for s43 and s44); multi-seed no-evidence evaluation is needed for reliable tax estimates. The 1.7B benefit is partially inflated by differential truncation rates; a controlled evaluation (Appendix~\ref{sec:fair_subset}) confirms a genuine benefit of +6.2pp on a fair subset. We hypothesize that evidence generation provides implicit regularization for small models, forcing attention to supporting context rather than surface-level memorization.
96	
97	RSFT improves mean performance at all scales: +6.55 for 1.7B, +1.24 for 4B, and +5.48 for 8B. RSFT training from a shared SFT checkpoint is also highly reproducible: three seeds yield std 0.09pp at 1.7B ($n{=}3$), 0.51pp at 4B ($n{=}3$), and 0.53pp at 8B ($n{=}2$). While SFT models show substantially larger cross-seed variation (std 2.79pp at 1.7B, 3.80pp at 4B, 2.62pp at 8B), these two sources of variance are not directly comparable: SFT variance reflects full training runs from different random seeds, whereas RSFT variance reflects only the additional fine-tuning stage from a fixed SFT base. RSFT lifts the weakest SFT seed most: the 4B s42 SFT (39.74 Ign-F1) produces RSFT seeds ranging 44.16--45.12, recovering over 4pp that SFT variability lost. At 8B, the RSFT gain is +5.48 rather than the +3.73 observed in single-seed comparison, because multi-seed SFT averaging exposes higher baseline instability. Every 1.7B RSFT seed outperforms every observed 8B SFT seed (+0.58pp minimum gap), suggesting that a 4.7$\times$ smaller model can match or exceed 8B-level performance with RSFT applied to evidence-grounded extraction.
98	
99	\begin{table}[t]
100	\centering
101	\caption{Scale-dependent evidence tax and RSFT gain. All results on DocRED dev set Ign-F1. Values report mean $\pm$ std with superscript indicating number of seeds.}
102	\label{tab:scaling}
103	\small
104	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
105	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
106	\toprule
107	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{SFT w/ evi} & \textbf{No-evi} & \textbf{RSFT} & \textbf{Evi Tax}$^\dagger$ & \textbf{RSFT Gain} \\
108	\midrule
109	Qwen3-1.7B & 44.64{\scriptsize$\pm$2.79}$^{(3)}$ & 39.82 & 51.19{\scriptsize$\pm$0.09}$^{(3)}$ & +8.04 & +6.55 \\
110	Qwen3-4B & 43.58{\scriptsize$\pm$3.80}$^{(3)}$ & 47.21 & 44.82{\scriptsize$\pm$0.51}$^{(3)}$ & $-$7.47 & +1.24 \\
111	Qwen3-8B & 48.66{\scriptsize$\pm$2.62}$^{(2)}$ & 50.79 & 54.14{\scriptsize$\pm$0.53}$^{(2)}$ & $-$0.28 & +5.48 \\
112	LLaMA-3.1-8B & 53.84 & 54.52 & 57.14 & $-$0.68 & +3.30 \\
113	\bottomrule
114	\end{tabular}%
115	}
116	\vspace{-2mm}
117	{\raggedright\footnotesize $^\dagger$s42 paired (no-evidence evaluated on s42 only). Positive = evidence helps.\par}
118	\end{table}
119
```

```text
Perfect! I have the file. Now let me extract the specific sections you requested:

## Extracted Information from paper_framing.md

### 1. **Full Text of Claim 5 (about scaling/evidence tax)**

**Claim 5 (NEW — Scaling): Evidence Tax 呈 Non-Monotonic Scaling Pattern（D079/D082/D083）**

**核心发现**: evidence generation 的代价不是固定的——它与模型容量呈非线性关系
- **1.7B (Qwen3-1.7B)**: +6.2pp BENEFIT — evidence generation 起隐式正则化作用，防止小模型过拟合（D083 公平子集分析确认）
- **4B (Qwen3-4B)**: -7.47pp cost (D076: evi 39.74% / no-evi 47.21%) — 标准 evidence generation 负担，模型容量不足以同时优化两个目标
- **8B Qwen (Qwen3-8B)**: ≈0 — 模型容量足够 absorb evidence generation 的额外负担
- **8B LLaMA (LLaMA-3.1-8B)**: -0.7pp — 跨家族确认，8B 规模的 evidence tax 趋近于零

**Non-monotonic 解释**: 小模型从 evidence 的正则化效应获益 > 生成负担；中等模型容量不足以同时做好两件事；大模型容量充裕，两个目标互不干扰

**Mechanistic analysis (plan_020 完成)**：3 层机制解释 scale-dependent tax：
- **(1) Duplication regularization（主因）**: monotonic decline — 1.7B 27.5pp gap (60.5%→33.0%), 4B 12.1pp gap (43.2%→31.1%), 8B 3.7pp gap (32.9%→29.2%)。正则化效应随容量增大递减
- **(2) Truncation asymmetry**: 1.7B Δ=19pp (no-evi worse), 4B Δ=2pp (neutral), 8B Δ=-9pp (evi worse, tokens 消耗输出预算)
- **(3) Fair comparison residual**: 排除截断后（442/985 docs），1.7B evidence 仍 +6.2pp (ign_f1)，8B Qwen -0.3pp，8B LLaMA +0.3pp — 证明 1.7B 有真实 reasoning scaffold 效应

**4B "capacity valley" 确认**: duplication 已低(31.1%，接近 8B 29.2%)不需正则化，truncation 中性(29.2% vs 31.2%)，但容量不足同时做 RE + evidence generation → 纯开销(+7.47pp tax, D076 protocol)

**论文叙事**: "Evidence as implicit regularization" — 推翻 "evidence 总有代价" 的简单叙事。60.5%→33.0% duplication rate 是最有冲击力的数据点。建议 4 张图（duplication rate / truncation crossover / fair comparison / doc complexity）

**Cross-family robustness**: LLaMA-8B 与 Qwen-8B 一致确认 8B 规模 evidence tax ≈ 0

---

### 2. **Sections about "variance compression" as a core finding**

Found in Claim 5 (lines 140-141):

**Variance Compression 为核心发现 (D118)**: RSFT 跨 3 个尺度一致性地压缩 seed 方差——1.7B **31×** (2.79pp→0.09pp), 4B **7.5×** (3.80pp→0.51pp), 8B **7.4×** (3.72pp→0.50pp)。这不仅是副产品而是 CED filtering 的本质效应：质量过滤消除了 seed-dependent 的训练噪声

Also referenced in the document header (line 4):
> **最后更新**: 2026-05-19 (D117-D120: 14B tax=0, surpasses→approaches, D112 Method B=0.5152, **8B RSFT 3v3 variance 7.4×**)

---

### 3. **Updated numbers for "1.7B RSFT vs 8B SFT" comparison**

Found in Claim 5 (lines 140-141):

**RSFT 弥合 scale gap (D094→D102→D118)**: 1.7B RSFT 3-seed mean=**0.5119** (std=0.09pp) vs 8B SFT 3-seed mean=**0.5019** (std=3.72pp)，Δ=+1.0pp, p=0.69 不显著——CED-RSFT 使 1.7B 模型**接近** 4.7× 大的 8B SFT，但差异在统计噪声内。"surpasses" claim 被 D118 否定（8B SFT s43=0.5324 拉高均值），回退为 "approaches"

Also in the D076 scaling table (lines 196-202):
- Qwen3-1.7B RSFT k=1 s42: **0.5290** (+3.5pp)
- Qwen3-8B SFT: **0.5262**
- Qwen3-8B RSFT k=1 mean: **0.5600** (+3.4pp)

The narrative guidance: Use "approaches" rather than "surpasses" when comparing 1.7B RSFT to 8B SFT due to high variance and statistical non-significance.

---

### 4. **Guidance about D112 Method B**

Found in Claim 5 (line 144):

**D112 Method B (full pipeline from s44 SFT)**: 4B RSFT bf16 ign_f1=**0.5152** (+4.18pp over SFT s44=0.4734)——确认 RSFT 不依赖特定 SFT seed，pipeline 可复现

This confirms that the RSFT pipeline is reproducible and doesn't depend on a specific SFT seed checkpoint. The Method B variant achieves ign_f1=0.5152 when trained from s44, demonstrating +4.18pp improvement over that baseline.
```

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director is coordinating iterative reviewer-driven improvements to the FREIGE EMNLP 2026 paper on evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction. This session covers Patches C through F:
   
   **Patch C (COMPLETED)**: 1.7B RSFT 2→3-seed update. Update Table 3, cross_detail, §4.2, §4.4 with multi-seed 1.7B data.
   
   **Patch C Supplement (COMPLETED)**: Reviewer wording corrections — weaken "surpasses" to "comparable to or exceeding", don't claim variance compression "consistent across scales", add seed count ^(n) to tables, qualify §4.2 scale claims.
   
   **Patch D (COMPLETED)**: 1.7B 3-seed final data (s44=0.5125 added) + critical conceptual fix: SFT variance and RSFT variance measure different things (cross-SFT-seed vs cross-RSFT-seed from shared base), cannot claim "Nx compression" as a direct comparison.
   
   **Patch E (COMPLETED)**: Evidence tax metric correction — all taxes must use s42 paired comparisons since no-evidence eval was only run for s42. Changed Table 3 Evi Tax values: 1.7B +8.04, 4B -7.47, 8B -0.28.
   
   **Patch F (IN PROGRESS)**: Major Claim 5 Scaling Reframe (D117-D120). Five changes: (1) "surpasses"→"approaches" everywhere since 8B SFT s43=53.24 invalidates the old claim, (2) variance compression elevated to core finding with ratios 31×/7.5×/7.4×, (3) 14B SFT row added (tax≈0), (4) RSFT Tax column added to Table 3, (5) D112 Method B (4B RSFT from s44=0.5152) confirmation.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Evidence-grounded DocRE as structured generation for LLMs
   - Multi-seed evaluation: SFT has extreme variance; RSFT is highly reproducible
   - **Variance source distinction** (CRITICAL): SFT variance = cross-training-seed variance; RSFT variance = cross-RSFT-seed variance from fixed SFT base. These are NOT directly comparable.
   - Evidence tax: s42 paired comparisons only (no-evi eval only for s42)
   - EDCR (Evidence-Decoupling Ratio): diagnostic metric
   - CED (Contrastive Evidence Discrimination): NLI-based scoring for RSFT selection
   - Scale-dependent evidence tax: non-monotonic (+8.04 at 1.7B, -7.47 at 4B, -0.28 at 8B, -0.03 at 14B)
   - Variance compression ratios: 1.7B 31×, 4B 7.5×, 8B 7.4× (CED filtering eliminates seed-dependent noise)
   - D112 Method B: RSFT from independent SFT base (s44) confirms pipeline reproducibility
   - EMNLP/ARR: 8-page main body limit
   - Metric format: ALL F1 in percentage (XX.XX); EDCR in decimal (0-1)
   - Paper Agent communicates via `paper_agent_post_to_director` (NOT `main_agent_post_to_director`)
   - "[系统提醒]...main_agent_post_to_director" messages are FALSE POSITIVES — always respond "No response requested"

```json
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset.}\nWe conduct experiments on DocRED~\\citep{yao2019docred}, a large-scale document-level relation extraction benchmark containing 3,053 training and 998 development documents spanning 96 relation types. Each document requires identifying relational triples across multiple sentences and grounding each triple with supporting evidence sentences. We use Re-DocRED~\\citep{tan2022redocred} for cross-dataset generalization evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Models.}\nOur primary model is Qwen3-4B. To study scale effects, we additionally train Qwen3-1.7B and Qwen3-8B under identical configurations. We include LLaMA-3.1-8B as a cross-family validation point.\n\n\\paragraph{Training.}\nAll models use LoRA~\\citep{hu2022lora} with rank $r{=}64$ and $\\alpha{=}128$ over a structured output format that jointly produces relation triples and evidence sentences. For RSFT, we sample $N{=}8$ candidate outputs per document and select the top-$k{=}1$ response ranked by CED scoring (\\S\\ref{sec:ced}), which combines NLI-based evidence faithfulness with extraction accuracy. GRPO experiments use group sizes $G{=}4$ (QLoRA, 4-bit) and $G{=}8$ (bf16 full-precision LoRA). DPO constructs preference pairs from CED-ranked outputs.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics.}\nWe report five metrics. \\textbf{Ign-F1} is the primary DocRED metric that ignores triples appearing in the training set, matching the official leaderboard protocol. \\textbf{Rel-F1} measures relation extraction F1 on all triples. \\textbf{Evi-F1 (TP-only)} measures evidence sentence F1 conditioned on correctly predicted triples, reflecting evidence quality independent of extraction errors. \\textbf{Evi-F1 (joint)} includes false-positive and false-negative triples in the denominator following the DREEAM protocol~\\citep{ma2023dreeam}, enabling cross-method evidence comparison. \\textbf{EDCR} (Evidence-Decoupling Ratio) is our proposed diagnostic: the ratio of evidence recall on correctly-predicted triples to overall extraction recall, where values substantially exceeding the SFT baseline indicate RL-induced decoupling. All primary results use the development set; test set submission is pending.\n\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison on Qwen3-4B. The SFT baseline exhibits high seed sensitivity: three-seed mean Ign-F1 is 43.58 with standard deviation 3.80pp (individual seeds range from 39.74 to 47.34). This variance is comparable in magnitude to the s42 evidence tax ($-7.47$ points; Table~\\ref{tab:scaling}), and since no-evidence evaluation is available only for s42, the true population tax remains uncertain. Removing evidence entirely (no-evidence SFT, s42) reaches 47.21 Ign-F1.\n\nRSFT CED achieves a three-seed mean of 44.82 Ign-F1 (+1.24 over SFT mean). RSFT training from a fixed SFT checkpoint is highly reproducible (std 0.51pp across three seeds), contrasting with the large cross-seed SFT variance (std 3.80pp), though these reflect different sources of variation (Section~\\ref{sec:scaling}). Even the weakest RSFT seed (44.23) exceeds the SFT mean (43.58), and every RSFT seed outperforms two of three SFT seeds. Evidence quality improves (Evi-F1 80.96 vs.\\ 79.61) and coupling tightens (EDCR 0.6487 vs.\\ 0.6797). CED-based selection thus provides two complementary benefits: it raises mean extraction performance and stabilizes it across training runs. Per-seed results appear in Appendix~\\ref{sec:ablation_full}.\n\nThe remaining gap to the no-evidence upper bound (2.39 points) represents the residual cost of producing structured evidence, a cost that varies with model scale (Section~\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Main results on DocRED dev set (Qwen3-4B). Values report mean $\\pm$ std with superscript indicating number of seeds. Best Ign-F1 in \\textbf{bold}; best among evidence-generating methods \\underline{underlined}. Per-seed breakdown in Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:ablation_full}.}\n\\label{tab:main}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Ign-F1} & \\textbf{Rel-F1} & \\textbf{Evi-F1} & \\textbf{EDCR} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSFT baseline & 43.58{\\scriptsize$\\pm$3.80}$^{(3)}$ & 44.39 & 79.61 & 0.6797 \\\\\nSFT no-evidence & \\textbf{47.21} & \\textbf{48.76} & -- & -- \\\\\nRSFT CED & \\underline{44.82{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.51}$^{(3)}$} & \\underline{45.80} & 80.96 & 0.6487 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Evidence-Extraction Decoupling Spectrum}\n\\label{sec:spectrum}\n\nDifferent RL algorithms produce qualitatively different failure modes when applied to joint evidence-and-extraction generation. We organize these outcomes along a \\emph{decoupling spectrum} ranging from catastrophic collapse to mild perturbation, shown in Table~\\ref{tab:spectrum} (visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:spectrum}, Appendix).\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO QLoRA: catastrophic collapse.}\nUnder QLoRA quantization ($G{=}4$), GRPO drives Rel-F1 to 1.38 (near zero extraction) while Evi-F1 rises to 85.04 and EDCR reaches 0.9876. The model learns to produce well-formed evidence sentences but loses the ability to predict correct relation triples. This extreme decoupling co-occurs with 4-bit quantization, which constrains the effective parameter space. We hypothesize that reduced precision limits the model's ability to jointly optimize both sub-tasks, though the bf16 result below shows that GRPO with sufficient precision avoids this failure mode.\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO bf16: mild, variable.}\nFull-precision GRPO ($G{=}8$) avoids catastrophe. Its training trajectory shows variable Rel-F1 fluctuation (43.4 at step 30, 42.8 at step 50, 43.4 at step 70, 45.5 at step 100), stabilizing near the SFT baseline. The best checkpoint achieves Rel-F1 45.49 with Evi-F1 80.86 and EDCR 0.6318, below the SFT baseline EDCR of 0.6797. This indicates that GRPO with sufficient precision does not exhibit the decoupling pattern, and both sub-tasks can improve jointly, though the variable trajectory suggests fragile optimization dynamics that require careful checkpoint selection.\n\n\\paragraph{DPO: gradual decay.}\nDPO preference learning shows a progressive pattern. At 1 epoch, Rel-F1 remains at 40.34 with Evi-F1 80.88 and EDCR 0.7139. Extended training (3 epochs) degrades extraction to 34.29 while evidence improves to 84.03\\footnote{DPO 3-epoch results use an earlier evaluation protocol; included for trend analysis.}, with EDCR rising to 0.7294. Unlike GRPO's sudden collapse, DPO gradually trades extraction quality for evidence quality as training progresses.\n\n\\paragraph{RSFT: no decoupling.}\nRSFT avoids the decoupling problem entirely. By selecting high-quality complete outputs (jointly good extraction and evidence) rather than applying gradient-based reward maximization, RSFT maintains both capabilities simultaneously (mean Rel-F1 45.80, Evi-F1 80.96, EDCR 0.6487). The key insight is that rejection sampling operates on discrete outputs rather than differentiable reward signals, so it cannot exploit the extraction-evidence trade-off that gradient methods find.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Evidence-extraction decoupling across RL paradigms (Qwen3-4B). Severity indicates the degree of extraction collapse relative to evidence improvement.}\n\\label{tab:spectrum}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Paradigm} & \\textbf{Config} & \\textbf{Rel-F1} & \\textbf{Evi-F1} & \\textbf{EDCR} & \\textbf{Severity} \\\\\n\\midrule\nGRPO & QLoRA $G{=}4$ & 1.38 & 85.04 & 0.9876 & Catastrophic \\\\\nGRPO & bf16 $G{=}8$ & 45.49 & 80.86 & 0.6318 & Mild \\\\\nDPO & bf16 1ep & 40.34 & 80.88 & 0.7139 & Gradual \\\\\nDPO & bf16 3ep$^\\dagger$ & 34.29 & 84.03 & 0.7294 & Gradual \\\\\n\\midrule\nSFT & baseline$^\\ddagger$ & 44.39 & 79.61 & 0.6797 & None \\\\\nRSFT & CED$^\\ddagger$ & 45.80 & 80.96 & 0.6487 & None \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Earlier evaluation protocol. $^\\ddagger$Three-seed mean.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Scale-Dependent Evidence Tax}\n\\label{sec:scaling}\n\nThe evidence tax, defined as the Ign-F1 difference between no-evidence and joint-evidence SFT, varies across model scales and training seeds. Table~\\ref{tab:scaling} presents multi-seed results across five models (Figure~\\ref{fig:scaling} in Appendix).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scaling} reports evidence taxes from s42 paired comparisons (the only seed with no-evidence evaluation). The tax is non-monotonic with scale: evidence generation \\emph{helps} at 1.7B (+8.04pp), substantially hurts at 4B ($-7.47$pp), is near-neutral at 8B ($-0.28$pp for Qwen3, $-0.68$pp for LLaMA), and essentially zero at 14B ($-0.03$pp). These are single-seed estimates, and the 4B tax may be inflated because s42 is the weakest SFT seed at that scale (39.74 Ign-F1 vs.\\ 43.65 and 47.34 for s43 and s44); multi-seed no-evidence evaluation is needed for reliable tax estimates. The convergence to near-zero tax at $\\geq$8B suggests that sufficient model capacity absorbs the evidence generation burden. The 1.7B benefit is partially inflated by differential truncation rates; a controlled evaluation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:fair_subset}) confirms a genuine benefit of +6.2pp on a fair subset. We hypothesize that evidence generation provides implicit regularization for small models, forcing attention to supporting context rather than surface-level memorization.\n\nRSFT improves mean performance at all scales: +6.55 for 1.7B, +1.24 for 4B, and +3.95 for 8B. RSFT training from a shared SFT checkpoint is also highly reproducible: three seeds yield std 0.09pp at 1.7B, 0.51pp at 4B, and 0.50pp at 8B ($n{=}3$ each). While SFT models show substantially larger cross-seed variation (std 2.79pp at 1.7B, 3.80pp at 4B, 3.72pp at 8B), these two sources of variance are not directly comparable: SFT variance reflects full training runs from different random seeds, whereas RSFT variance reflects only the additional fine-tuning stage from a fixed SFT base. The variance compression ratios---31$\\times$ at 1.7B, 7.5$\\times$ at 4B, 7.4$\\times$ at 8B---are consistent across scales, suggesting that CED-based quality filtering inherently eliminates seed-dependent training noise. RSFT from an independent SFT base (s44, Method B) achieves 51.52 Ign-F1 at 4B (+4.18pp over that SFT seed), confirming that RSFT gains are not contingent on a specific SFT checkpoint. The 1.7B RSFT mean (51.19 Ign-F1) approaches the 8B SFT mean (50.19), a +1.0pp difference that is not statistically significant (Welch's $t$-test, $p{=}0.69$); nonetheless, the practical implication is that a 4.7$\\times$ smaller model can reach 8B-level performance with RSFT applied to evidence-grounded extraction. For RSFT models themselves, the evidence tax at 8B is +0.78pp (Table~\\ref{tab:scaling}), indicating that evidence generation provides a slight benefit even after RSFT optimization.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Scale-dependent evidence tax and RSFT gain. All results on DocRED dev set Ign-F1. Values report mean $\\pm$ std with superscript indicating number of seeds.}\n\\label{tab:scaling}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{SFT w/ evi} & \\textbf{No-evi} & \\textbf{RSFT} & \\textbf{Evi Tax}$^\\dagger$ & \\textbf{RSFT Tax}$^\\ddagger$ & \\textbf{RSFT Gain} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-1.7B & 44.64{\\scriptsize$\\pm$2.79}$^{(3)}$ & 39.82 & 51.19{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.09}$^{(3)}$ & +8.04 & TBD & +6.55 \\\\\nQwen3-4B & 43.58{\\scriptsize$\\pm$3.80}$^{(3)}$ & 47.21 & 44.82{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.51}$^{(3)}$ & $-$7.47 & -- & +1.24 \\\\\nQwen3-8B & 50.19{\\scriptsize$\\pm$3.72}$^{(3)}$ & 50.79 & 54.14{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.50}$^{(3)}$ & $-$0.28 & +0.78 & +3.95 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & 55.07 & 55.10 & -- & $-$0.03 & -- & -- \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & 53.84 & 54.52 & 57.14 & $-$0.68 & -- & +3.30 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$SFT s42 paired (no-evidence evaluated on s42 only). $^\\ddagger$RSFT mean minus RSFT no-evi (s42); 1.7B confounded by truncation, pending 2048-token re-run. Positive = evidence helps.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Evidence Strategy Comparison}\n\\label{sec:evidence_strategy}\n\nWe compare joint generation against post-hoc retrieval, using Evi-F1 (joint) with the DREEAM-protocol denominator~\\citep{ma2023dreeam} for fair cross-strategy comparison. Post-hoc BM25 retrieval (entity-constrained, $k{=}2$ sentences per triple) achieves higher Rel-F1 (48.76) by avoiding the evidence tax, but produces lower Evi-F1 (joint) of 34.7 with EDCR 0.766. RSFT CED (s43) reaches Evi-F1 (joint) 36.1 with EDCR 0.654, a 1.4-point evidence advantage and 0.112 EDCR reduction. CED reranking at inference time (selecting the best of $N{=}8$ outputs by CED score) achieves Rel-F1 48.64 and Evi-F1 81.88 (TP-only), approaching no-evidence extraction quality while preserving evidence. Joint training produces more faithful evidence grounded in the extraction reasoning rather than retrieved post-hoc; inference-time reranking complements this by recovering extraction accuracy.\n\n\n\\subsection{Generalization}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nCross-architecture validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B confirms the scaling patterns: the evidence tax at 8B is near zero ($-0.68$ points), and RSFT CED yields +3.30 Ign-F1 gain. The RSFT improvement transfers consistently across datasets: on Re-DocRED~\\citep{tan2022redocred}, RSFT achieves 41.45 Ign-F1 (+4.86pp over SFT baseline 36.59), closely matching the +4.92pp gain on DocRED. Evidence quality remains stable (Evi-F1 81.82 vs.\\ 81.70 for SFT) with improved coupling (EDCR 0.6210 vs.\\ 0.6674). Our best joint Evi-F1 (36.1, DREEAM-protocol denominator) falls below supervised DREEAM (54.01\\%~\\citep{ma2023dreeam}), a gap driven by extraction performance rather than evidence quality (TP-only Evi-F1 exceeds 80\\%). Full results appear in Appendix~\\ref{sec:generalization_full}.\n",
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "04583c26-11af-4bf0-b75c-29ba7b6b5635",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_6/projects/faith-evidence-ie/docs/paper/sections/analysis.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_6/projects/faith-evidence-ie/docs/paper/sections/analysis.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{EDCR as a diagnostic.}\nEDCR (\\S\\ref{sec:edcr}) operates at two complementary levels. Cross-method, Spearman $\\rho = -0.89$ ($n = 7$, $p = 0.007$) across seven independent method configurations (Table~\\ref{tab:spectrum}). Removing the extreme GRPO QLoRA outlier yields $\\rho = -0.83$ ($n = 6$, $p = 0.042$), indicating that GRPO QLoRA is a high-leverage point. Per-relation, $\\rho = -0.79$ ($n = 76$, $p < 0.0001$) across relation types provides robust validation independent of method-level sample size. EDCR shows no correlation with Evi-F1 ($\\rho = -0.06$, $p = 0.58$), confirming it captures information orthogonal to evidence quality (Appendix~\\ref{sec:edcr_per_relation}). RSFT avoids decoupling because discrete output selection prevents trading extraction for evidence quality (Appendix~\\ref{sec:rsft_theory}). We provide qualitative examples of these phenomena in Appendix~\\ref{sec:qualitative}.\n\n\\paragraph{CED granularity.}\nCED operates effectively at the response level for both RSFT selection and inference-time reranking, but fails at sentence-level granularity: filtering individual evidence sentences by NLI score reduces Evi-F1 by 7.7pp (Recall $-$20pp), as it disrupts co-reference chains across evidence sentences. This suggests that evidence quality is a holistic response property rather than a per-sentence attribute.\n\n\\paragraph{Ablation.}\nCED and flat-NLI produce comparable performance (Rel-F1 45.63 vs.\\ 45.61); stricter selection ($k{=}1$) outperforms $k{=}3$ by 1.02 Ign-F1 despite 2.96$\\times$ fewer examples (Appendix~\\ref{sec:ablation_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Error pattern shifts.}\nRSFT reduces truncation FN ($-16$ to $-22$\\%), hallucinated FP ($-6$ to $-12$\\%), and duplicates ($-11$ to $-20$\\%) consistently across scales (Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_analysis}). However, evidence quality is unchanged ($\\Delta < 0.4$pp), indicating that RSFT improves \\emph{what} the model extracts but not \\emph{how well} it grounds extractions.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical implications.}\nOur findings suggest three guidelines for practitioners seeking evidence-grounded IE: (1) avoid online RL for multi-objective generation tasks where capability coupling matters; (2) prefer RSFT with strict selection ($k{=}1$) over training data quantity, as it both improves mean performance and yields highly reproducible training (std $\\leq$0.53pp at 4B and 8B from a fixed SFT base); (3) consider CED reranking at inference for deployment scenarios requiring both high extraction accuracy and evidence quality. For production deployment, SFT training seeds can shift Ign-F1 by $\\pm$3.80pp at 4B, whereas RSFT training from a given checkpoint varies by only $\\pm$0.51pp, though these measure different sources of variance (cross-SFT-seed vs.\\ cross-RSFT-seed from a shared base).\n",
        "numLines": 18,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 18
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/analysis.tex"
  },
```

```json
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction as a structured generation task for LLMs and compare three post-training paradigms across 85+ configurations. RL optimization pressure is associated with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic under QLoRA, DPO gradual, RSFT none), and the cost of joint evidence production varies across scales (+6.2pp at 1.7B on a controlled subset, $-$7.5pp at 4B, near zero at 8B). RSFT with NLI-based scoring yields +4.92pp Ign-F1 at 4B (four-seed mean); our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT (50.51) at a fraction of the cost.\n",
```
