```json
{
  "parentUuid": "bfb2f512-27c2-4876-85ed-3a7aa01c279b",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_6/projects/faith-evidence-ie/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_6/projects/faith-evidence-ie/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nDocument-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \\citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.\nRecent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \\citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition for end-to-end extraction, \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.\nThese methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \\citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy.\nYet every one of them outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without any indication of \\emph{which sentences} in the document support each prediction.\nIn deployment contexts where predictions feed into clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, or financial compliance systems, end users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.\n\nThe absence of verifiable evidence creates a practical bottleneck.\nRelation extraction outputs are consumed downstream by knowledge graph construction and human decision workflows.\nWhen a system predicts that a drug interacts with a gene, or that a contract clause implies a liability, practitioners must trace each prediction back to source text before acting on it.\nWithout machine-generated evidence, this verification requires exhaustive manual review of the input document for every predicted relation.\nEncoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \\citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \\citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.\nBoth improve extraction quality \\emph{and} produce evidence annotations.\nHowever, encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in extraction accuracy, and their evidence mechanisms (binary sentence classification) do not transfer to generative architectures.\nThe community therefore faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while the evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.\n\nThis gap reflects a deeper structural difference between evidence in encoder and generative frameworks.\nIn SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is a binary classification task (does sentence $s_i$ support relation $r$?) that serves as auxiliary supervision.\nThe evidence signal \\emph{regularizes} the encoder and improves extraction.\nIn LLM-based DocRE, evidence-grounded extraction requires the model to \\emph{generate} complete evidence sentences as part of its output sequence, substantially increasing output length and decoding complexity.\nEvidence production in this setting is not an auxiliary signal but an additional generation burden that competes with the extraction objective for model capacity.\nWhether this generation cost necessarily harms extraction, or whether the interaction is more nuanced, remains an open question: the answer may depend on model scale and optimization strategy.\nThis uncertainty motivates a systematic investigation of how evidence production interacts with extraction quality across model scales and optimization paradigms.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task for LLMs and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: online reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \\citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}), offline preference optimization via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO; \\citealt{rafailov2023direct}), and offline reinforcement learning via Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT, building on STaR; \\citealt{zelikman2022star}).\nOur experiments across 85+ configurations, spanning four model scales (1.7B to 8B), two model families, and two datasets, reveal three principal findings.\n\\textbf{First}, we identify a \\emph{decoupling spectrum}: the strength of RL optimization pressure correlates with the severity of evidence-extraction decoupling.\nGRPO with Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA; \\citealt{hu2022lora}) produces catastrophic decoupling where extraction accuracy collapses to near zero; GRPO with full-precision training shows mild decoupling; DPO exhibits gradual degradation; and RSFT maintains stable joint performance.\nCatastrophic extraction collapse co-occurs with QLoRA-based GRPO training, though we note that GRPO with full-precision training (bf16, $G{=}8$) achieves EDCR below the SFT baseline, indicating that the decoupling is not inherent to online RL when sufficient precision is available.\n\\textbf{Second}, we discover a \\emph{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of jointly producing evidence and relations varies across model scales.\nAt 1.7B parameters, evidence generation provides an implicit regularization benefit of +6.2pp Ign-F1 on a controlled evaluation subset; at 4B, it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty; at 8B, the tax approaches zero.\nThis pattern challenges the assumption that evidence generation uniformly harms extraction.\n\\textbf{Third}, RSFT with NLI-based scoring for training data selection emerges as the most effective optimization route.\nBy selecting high-quality training samples through output filtering rather than applying gradient-based optimization pressure, RSFT sidesteps the multi-objective competition that destabilizes RL and preference methods.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We formalize \\textbf{evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction} as a generation task for LLMs and conduct the first \\textbf{comprehensive empirical study} spanning 85+ experiments across four model scales, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms.\n\n    \\item We characterize a \\textbf{decoupling spectrum} across optimization paradigms: RL optimization pressure is associated with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We use the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR), a diagnostic that tracks misalignment between extraction and evidence capabilities, as an analytical tool throughout our study. EDCR shares Rel-Recall with Rel-F1 by construction; we interpret the observed cross-method correlation ($\\rho = -0.89$, $n = 7$) as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent finding, and validate through per-relation analysis ($\\rho = -0.79$, $n = 76$) that avoids the shared-component concern.\n\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of joint evidence production varies across 1.7B, 4B, and 8B parameters, challenging the assumption that evidence generation uniformly degrades extraction quality.\n\n    \\item We establish \\textbf{RSFT with NLI-based scoring} as an effective solution, using CED or flat-NLI to select high-quality training data. RSFT achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over SFT at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT performance (50.51), enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction at reduced computational cost.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 47,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 47
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex"
  },
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QXKQcWshHHDKHSnt3zE5Er`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	
3	Large Language Model (LLM) based Document-level Relation Extraction (DocRE) methods now match or exceed encoder baselines in extraction accuracy, yet they produce bare relation triplets without identifying the sentences that support each prediction.
4	In deployment contexts such as clinical knowledge bases and legal due-diligence systems, users cannot verify predictions without manually locating textual evidence across multi-page documents.
5	Encoder-based evidence methods (SAIS, DREEAM) can produce supporting sentences but trail LLM approaches in accuracy. The community therefore faces a gap between extractive precision and output verifiability.
6	
7	We formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT).
8	Our study spans 85+ experimental configurations across four model scales (1.7B to 8B parameters), two model families, and two datasets.
9	We use the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic tool and NLI-based scoring for sample selection.
10	
11	Three findings emerge.
12	First, RL optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity: GRPO causes catastrophic decoupling, DPO shows gradual degradation, and RSFT preserves stable joint performance.
13	Second, the cost of evidence production varies across model scales: at 1.7B parameters evidence provides a +6.2pp Ign-F1 benefit on a controlled evaluation subset excluding truncated and repetitive outputs, at 4B it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty, and at 8B the cost approaches zero.
14	Third, RSFT with NLI-based scoring is the most effective route: it achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over supervised fine-tuning at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT performance (50.51).
15	
16	\end{abstract}
17	
```

The community therefore faces a gap between extractive precision and output verifiability.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT).\nOur study spans 85+ experimental configurations across four model scales (1.7B to 8B parameters), two model families, and two datasets.\nWe use the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic tool and NLI-based scoring for sample selection.\n\nThree findings emerge.\nFirst, RL optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity: GRPO causes catastrophic decoupling, DPO shows gradual degradation, and RSFT preserves stable joint performance.\nSecond, the cost of evidence production varies across model scales: at 1.7B parameters evidence provides a +6.2pp Ign-F1 benefit on a controlled evaluation subset excluding truncated and repetitive outputs, at 4B it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty, and at 8B the cost approaches zero.\nThird, RSFT with NLI-based scoring is the most effective route: it achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over supervised fine-tuning at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT performance (50.51).\n\n\\end{abstract}\n",

```json
    "oldString": "Our study spans 85+ experimental configurations across four model scales (1.7B to 8B parameters), two model families, and two datasets.",
    "newString": "Our study spans 85+ experimental configurations across five model scales (1.7B to 14B parameters), two model families, and two datasets.",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\n\nLarge Language Model (LLM) based Document-level Relation Extraction (DocRE) methods now match or exceed encoder baselines in extraction accuracy, yet they produce bare relation triplets without identifying the sentences that support each prediction.\nIn deployment contexts such as clinical knowledge bases and legal due-diligence systems, users cannot verify predictions without manually locating textual evidence across multi-page documents.\nEncoder-based evidence methods (SAIS, DREEAM) can produce supporting sentences but trail LLM approaches in accuracy. The community therefore faces a gap between extractive precision and output verifiability.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT).\nOur study spans 85+ experimental configurations across four model scales (1.7B to 8B parameters), two model families, and two datasets.\nWe use the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR) as a diagnostic tool and NLI-based scoring for sample selection.\n\nThree findings emerge.\nFirst, RL optimization pressure correlates with evidence-extraction decoupling severity: GRPO causes catastrophic decoupling, DPO shows gradual degradation, and RSFT preserves stable joint performance.\nSecond, the cost of evidence production varies across model scales: at 1.7B parameters evidence provides a +6.2pp Ign-F1 benefit on a controlled evaluation subset excluding truncated and repetitive outputs, at 4B it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty, and at 8B the cost approaches zero.\nThird, RSFT with NLI-based scoring is the most effective route: it achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over supervised fine-tuning at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT performance (50.51).\n\n\\end{abstract}\n",
```

```json
          " Second, the cost of evidence production varies across model scales: at 1.7B parameters evidence provides a +6.2pp Ign-F1 benefit on a controlled evaluation subset excluding truncated and repetitive outputs, at 4B it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty, and at $\\geq$8B the cost approaches zero.",
          "-Third, RSFT with NLI-based scoring is the most effective route: it achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over supervised fine-tuning at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT performance (50.51).",
          "+Third, RSFT with NLI-based scoring is the most effective route: it improves mean Ign-F1 at all scales (+1.2 to +6.6pp) with highly reproducible training (variance compression of 7--31$\\times$ across scales), and our 1.7B RSFT model (51.19 Ign-F1) approaches 8B SFT performance (50.19) at a fraction of the computational cost.",
          " ",
          " \\end{abstract}"
        ]
```

```json
    "oldString": "Our experiments across 85+ configurations, spanning four model scales (1.7B to 8B), two model families, and two datasets, reveal three principal findings.",
    "newString": "Our experiments across 85+ configurations, spanning five model scales (1.7B to 14B), two model families, and two datasets, reveal three principal findings.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nDocument-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \\citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.\nRecent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \\citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition for end-to-end extraction, \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.\nThese methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \\citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy.\nYet every one of them outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without any indication of \\emph{which sentences} in the document support each prediction.\nIn deployment contexts where predictions feed into clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, or financial compliance systems, end users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.\n\nThe absence of verifiable evidence creates a practical bottleneck.\nRelation extraction outputs are consumed downstream by knowledge graph construction and human decision workflows.\nWhen a system predicts that a drug interacts with a gene, or that a contract clause implies a liability, practitioners must trace each prediction back to source text before acting on it.\nWithout machine-generated evidence, this verification requires exhaustive manual review of the input document for every predicted relation.\nEncoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \\citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \\citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.\nBoth improve extraction quality \\emph{and} produce evidence annotations.\nHowever, encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in extraction accuracy, and their evidence mechanisms (binary sentence classification) do not transfer to generative architectures.\nThe community therefore faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while the evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.\n\nThis gap reflects a deeper structural difference between evidence in encoder and generative frameworks.\nIn SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is a binary classification task (does sentence $s_i$ support relation $r$?) that serves as auxiliary supervision.\nThe evidence signal \\emph{regularizes} the encoder and improves extraction.\nIn LLM-based DocRE, evidence-grounded extraction requires the model to \\emph{generate} complete evidence sentences as part of its output sequence, substantially increasing output length and decoding complexity.\nEvidence production in this setting is not an auxiliary signal but an additional generation burden that competes with the extraction objective for model capacity.\nWhether this generation cost necessarily harms extraction, or whether the interaction is more nuanced, remains an open question: the answer may depend on model scale and optimization strategy.\nThis uncertainty motivates a systematic investigation of how evidence production interacts with extraction quality across model scales and optimization paradigms.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task for LLMs and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: online reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \\citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}), offline preference optimization via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO; \\citealt{rafailov2023direct}), and offline reinforcement learning via Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT, building on STaR; \\citealt{zelikman2022star}).\nOur experiments across 85+ configurations, spanning four model scales (1.7B to 8B), two model families, and two datasets, reveal three principal findings.\n\\textbf{First}, we identify a \\emph{decoupling spectrum}: the strength of RL optimization pressure correlates with the severity of evidence-extraction decoupling.\nGRPO with Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA; \\citealt{hu2022lora}) produces catastrophic decoupling where extraction accuracy collapses to near zero; GRPO with full-precision training shows mild decoupling; DPO exhibits gradual degradation; and RSFT maintains stable joint performance.\nCatastrophic extraction collapse co-occurs with QLoRA-based GRPO training, though we note that GRPO with full-precision training (bf16, $G{=}8$) achieves EDCR below the SFT baseline, indicating that the decoupling is not inherent to online RL when sufficient precision is available.\n\\textbf{Second}, we discover a \\emph{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of jointly producing evidence and relations varies across model scales.\nAt 1.7B parameters, evidence generation provides an implicit regularization benefit of +6.2pp Ign-F1 on a controlled evaluation subset; at 4B, it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty; at 8B, the tax approaches zero.\nThis pattern challenges the assumption that evidence generation uniformly harms extraction.\n\\textbf{Third}, RSFT with NLI-based scoring for training data selection emerges as the most effective optimization route.\nBy selecting high-quality training samples through output filtering rather than applying gradient-based optimization pressure, RSFT sidesteps the multi-objective competition that destabilizes RL and preference methods.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We formalize \\textbf{evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction} as a generation task for LLMs and conduct the first \\textbf{comprehensive empirical study} spanning 85+ experiments across four model scales, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms.\n\n    \\item We characterize a \\textbf{decoupling spectrum} across optimization paradigms: RL optimization pressure is associated with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We use the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR), a diagnostic that tracks misalignment between extraction and evidence capabilities, as an analytical tool throughout our study. EDCR shares Rel-Recall with Rel-F1 by construction; we interpret the observed cross-method correlation ($\\rho = -0.89$, $n = 7$) as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent finding, and validate through per-relation analysis ($\\rho = -0.79$, $n = 76$) that avoids the shared-component concern.\n\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of joint evidence production varies across 1.7B, 4B, and 8B parameters, challenging the assumption that evidence generation uniformly degrades extraction quality.\n\n    \\item We establish \\textbf{RSFT with NLI-based scoring} as an effective solution, using CED or flat-NLI to select high-quality training data. RSFT achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over SFT at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT performance (50.51), enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction at reduced computational cost.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
```

```json
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_6/projects/faith-evidence-ie/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
```

```json
    "newString": "spanning 85+ experiments across five model scales, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nDocument-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \\citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.\nRecent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \\citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition for end-to-end extraction, \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.\nThese methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \\citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy.\nYet every one of them outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without any indication of \\emph{which sentences} in the document support each prediction.\nIn deployment contexts where predictions feed into clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, or financial compliance systems, end users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.\n\nThe absence of verifiable evidence creates a practical bottleneck.\nRelation extraction outputs are consumed downstream by knowledge graph construction and human decision workflows.\nWhen a system predicts that a drug interacts with a gene, or that a contract clause implies a liability, practitioners must trace each prediction back to source text before acting on it.\nWithout machine-generated evidence, this verification requires exhaustive manual review of the input document for every predicted relation.\nEncoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \\citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \\citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.\nBoth improve extraction quality \\emph{and} produce evidence annotations.\nHowever, encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in extraction accuracy, and their evidence mechanisms (binary sentence classification) do not transfer to generative architectures.\nThe community therefore faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while the evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.\n\nThis gap reflects a deeper structural difference between evidence in encoder and generative frameworks.\nIn SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is a binary classification task (does sentence $s_i$ support relation $r$?) that serves as auxiliary supervision.\nThe evidence signal \\emph{regularizes} the encoder and improves extraction.\nIn LLM-based DocRE, evidence-grounded extraction requires the model to \\emph{generate} complete evidence sentences as part of its output sequence, substantially increasing output length and decoding complexity.\nEvidence production in this setting is not an auxiliary signal but an additional generation burden that competes with the extraction objective for model capacity.\nWhether this generation cost necessarily harms extraction, or whether the interaction is more nuanced, remains an open question: the answer may depend on model scale and optimization strategy.\nThis uncertainty motivates a systematic investigation of how evidence production interacts with extraction quality across model scales and optimization paradigms.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task for LLMs and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: online reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \\citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}), offline preference optimization via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO; \\citealt{rafailov2023direct}), and offline reinforcement learning via Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT, building on STaR; \\citealt{zelikman2022star}).\nOur experiments across 85+ configurations, spanning five model scales (1.7B to 14B), two model families, and two datasets, reveal three principal findings.\n\\textbf{First}, we identify a \\emph{decoupling spectrum}: the strength of RL optimization pressure correlates with the severity of evidence-extraction decoupling.\nGRPO with Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA; \\citealt{hu2022lora}) produces catastrophic decoupling where extraction accuracy collapses to near zero; GRPO with full-precision training shows mild decoupling; DPO exhibits gradual degradation; and RSFT maintains stable joint performance.\nCatastrophic extraction collapse co-occurs with QLoRA-based GRPO training, though we note that GRPO with full-precision training (bf16, $G{=}8$) achieves EDCR below the SFT baseline, indicating that the decoupling is not inherent to online RL when sufficient precision is available.\n\\textbf{Second}, we discover a \\emph{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of jointly producing evidence and relations varies across model scales.\nAt 1.7B parameters, evidence generation provides an implicit regularization benefit of +6.2pp Ign-F1 on a controlled evaluation subset; at 4B, it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty; at $\\geq$8B, the tax approaches zero.\nThis pattern challenges the assumption that evidence generation uniformly harms extraction.\n\\textbf{Third}, RSFT with NLI-based scoring for training data selection emerges as the most effective optimization route.\nBy selecting high-quality training samples through output filtering rather than applying gradient-based optimization pressure, RSFT sidesteps the multi-objective competition that destabilizes RL and preference methods.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We formalize \\textbf{evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction} as a generation task for LLMs and conduct the first \\textbf{comprehensive empirical study} spanning 85+ experiments across four model scales, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms.\n\n    \\item We characterize a \\textbf{decoupling spectrum} across optimization paradigms: RL optimization pressure is associated with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We use the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR), a diagnostic that tracks misalignment between extraction and evidence capabilities, as an analytical tool throughout our study. EDCR shares Rel-Recall with Rel-F1 by construction; we interpret the observed cross-method correlation ($\\rho = -0.89$, $n = 7$) as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent finding, and validate through per-relation analysis ($\\rho = -0.79$, $n = 76$) that avoids the shared-component concern.\n\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of joint evidence production varies across 1.7B, 4B, and 8B parameters, challenging the assumption that evidence generation uniformly degrades extraction quality.\n\n    \\item We establish \\textbf{RSFT with NLI-based scoring} as an effective solution, using CED or flat-NLI to select high-quality training data. RSFT achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over SFT at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT performance (50.51), enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction at reduced computational cost.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nDocument-level relation extraction (DocRE) requires identifying semantic relations between entity pairs across multiple sentences, a task where encoder-based methods such as \\citet{zhou2021atlop} long dominated.\nRecent LLM-based approaches have closed this gap: \\citet{autore2024} fine-tune LLMs with structured decomposition for end-to-end extraction, \\citet{xu2024lmrc} frame it as multi-choice reading comprehension, and \\citet{chen2025docksrag} combine retrieval-augmented generation with structured knowledge.\nThese methods approach or surpass encoder baselines on DocRED \\citep{yao2019docred} and Re-DocRED \\citep{tan2022redocred} in extraction accuracy.\nYet every one of them outputs bare relation triplets $(e_h, r, e_t)$ without any indication of \\emph{which sentences} in the document support each prediction.\nIn deployment contexts where predictions feed into clinical knowledge bases, legal due-diligence pipelines, or financial compliance systems, end users cannot verify a prediction without manually locating its textual basis across a multi-page document.\n\nThe absence of verifiable evidence creates a practical bottleneck.\nRelation extraction outputs are consumed downstream by knowledge graph construction and human decision workflows.\nWhen a system predicts that a drug interacts with a gene, or that a contract clause implies a liability, practitioners must trace each prediction back to source text before acting on it.\nWithout machine-generated evidence, this verification requires exhaustive manual review of the input document for every predicted relation.\nEncoder-based methods have partially addressed this need: \\citet{xiao2022sais} introduce sentence-level evidence supervision as an auxiliary signal, and \\citet{ma2023dreeam} extend this with dynamic evidence retrieval through memory attention.\nBoth improve extraction quality \\emph{and} produce evidence annotations.\nHowever, encoder methods now trail LLM-based approaches in extraction accuracy, and their evidence mechanisms (binary sentence classification) do not transfer to generative architectures.\nThe community therefore faces a gap: the most accurate extractors provide no evidence, while the evidence-capable methods are no longer competitive.\n\nThis gap reflects a deeper structural difference between evidence in encoder and generative frameworks.\nIn SAIS and DREEAM, evidence retrieval is a binary classification task (does sentence $s_i$ support relation $r$?) that serves as auxiliary supervision.\nThe evidence signal \\emph{regularizes} the encoder and improves extraction.\nIn LLM-based DocRE, evidence-grounded extraction requires the model to \\emph{generate} complete evidence sentences as part of its output sequence, substantially increasing output length and decoding complexity.\nEvidence production in this setting is not an auxiliary signal but an additional generation burden that competes with the extraction objective for model capacity.\nWhether this generation cost necessarily harms extraction, or whether the interaction is more nuanced, remains an open question: the answer may depend on model scale and optimization strategy.\nThis uncertainty motivates a systematic investigation of how evidence production interacts with extraction quality across model scales and optimization paradigms.\n\nWe formalize evidence-grounded DocRE as a structured generation task for LLMs and conduct a systematic comparison of three post-training optimization paradigms: online reinforcement learning via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO; \\citealt{shao2024deepseekmath}), offline preference optimization via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO; \\citealt{rafailov2023direct}), and offline reinforcement learning via Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning (RSFT, building on STaR; \\citealt{zelikman2022star}).\nOur experiments across 85+ configurations, spanning five model scales (1.7B to 14B), two model families, and two datasets, reveal three principal findings.\n\\textbf{First}, we identify a \\emph{decoupling spectrum}: the strength of RL optimization pressure correlates with the severity of evidence-extraction decoupling.\nGRPO with Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA; \\citealt{hu2022lora}) produces catastrophic decoupling where extraction accuracy collapses to near zero; GRPO with full-precision training shows mild decoupling; DPO exhibits gradual degradation; and RSFT maintains stable joint performance.\nCatastrophic extraction collapse co-occurs with QLoRA-based GRPO training, though we note that GRPO with full-precision training (bf16, $G{=}8$) achieves EDCR below the SFT baseline, indicating that the decoupling is not inherent to online RL when sufficient precision is available.\n\\textbf{Second}, we discover a \\emph{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of jointly producing evidence and relations varies across model scales.\nAt 1.7B parameters, evidence generation provides an implicit regularization benefit of +6.2pp Ign-F1 on a controlled evaluation subset; at 4B, it imposes a $-$7.5pp penalty; at $\\geq$8B, the tax approaches zero.\nThis pattern challenges the assumption that evidence generation uniformly harms extraction.\n\\textbf{Third}, RSFT with NLI-based scoring for training data selection emerges as the most effective optimization route.\nBy selecting high-quality training samples through output filtering rather than applying gradient-based optimization pressure, RSFT sidesteps the multi-objective competition that destabilizes RL and preference methods.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We formalize \\textbf{evidence-grounded document-level relation extraction} as a generation task for LLMs and conduct the first \\textbf{comprehensive empirical study} spanning 85+ experiments across five model scales, two model families, two datasets (DocRED, Re-DocRED), and three optimization paradigms.\n\n    \\item We characterize a \\textbf{decoupling spectrum} across optimization paradigms: RL optimization pressure is associated with evidence-extraction decoupling severity (GRPO catastrophic $\\rightarrow$ DPO gradual $\\rightarrow$ RSFT none). We use the Evidence-Decoupling Ratio (EDCR), a diagnostic that tracks misalignment between extraction and evidence capabilities, as an analytical tool throughout our study. EDCR shares Rel-Recall with Rel-F1 by construction; we interpret the observed cross-method correlation ($\\rho = -0.89$, $n = 7$) as a diagnostic pattern rather than an independent finding, and validate through per-relation analysis ($\\rho = -0.79$, $n = 76$) that avoids the shared-component concern.\n\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{scale-dependent evidence tax}: the cost of joint evidence production varies across 1.7B, 4B, 8B, and 14B parameters, challenging the assumption that evidence generation uniformly degrades extraction quality.\n\n    \\item We establish \\textbf{RSFT with NLI-based scoring} as an effective solution, using CED or flat-NLI to select high-quality training data. RSFT achieves +4.92pp Ign-F1 over SFT at 4B scale (four-seed mean), and our 1.7B RSFT model (51.22 Ign-F1) surpasses 8B SFT performance (50.51), enabling deployment-efficient evidence-grounded extraction at reduced computational cost.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
```
